4. Discussion

In this study, we investigated a wide range of speech-accompanying conversational facial signals in a rich corpus of dyadic Dutch multimodal face-to-face interactions. We asked how the production of different facial signals mapped onto the communication of two fundamental social actions in conversation, questions and responses, by looking at their proportional distribution, clustering, and timing with regard to verbal utterance onset. Results showed a high proportion of facial signals being used, with, despite some overlap, a qualitatively different distribution in questions versus responses. Additionally, clusters of facial signals were identified within questions and responses. Importantly, most facial signals occurred early in the utterance, and had earlier onsets in questions than in responses. Below we discuss these findings in turn.

## Distribution of Facial Signals across Questions and Responses

When looking at the first proportion of all question- or response-related facial signals, facial signals were more likely to co-occur with questions than with responses. It could be that questions are more visually marked because of a larger urgency for the listener to recognise the message fast to provide an appropriate answer, since long gaps indicate a dispreferred response \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B8-brainsci-11-01017

\]. Therefore, facial signals may facilitate the recognition of social actions such as questions early, which in turn may help potential following speakers to understand the intended message quickly and plan a timely response \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B9-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B10-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B11-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B12-brainsci-11-01017

\]. This result thus demonstrates that facial signals appear to form a core element of signalling speaker intentions in conversational social interaction.

Two particular interesting findings in regards to the distribution of facial signals in questions and responses are worth noting. The finding that there were more eyebrow movements in questions compared to responses is in line with past studies showing links between eyebrow movements and questions in spoken and signed languages \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B21-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B22-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B23-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B32-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B33-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B42-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B46-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B48-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B49-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B50-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B51-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B52-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B53-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B54-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B55-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B56-brainsci-11-01017

\]. This finding therefore supports the notion that eyebrow movements signal the intention to pose a question. A second notable finding is that, while most facial signals were more likely to occur in questions than responses, there was one exception: pressed lips had a higher proportion in responses compared to questions. This signal forms part of the not-face \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B37-brainsci-11-01017

\], suggesting that this facial expression may be more likely to be used to express negation or disagreement in responses.

Interestingly, when looking at the proportion of questions and responses that contained at least one of the facial signals analysed here, responses were more likely than questions to contain a facial signal, with the exception of eyebrow frowns and squints. This difference between the two proportion analyses for the distribution of facial signals in questions and responses indicates that there may be multiple facial signals per verbal utterance. In the first proportion analysis, we calculated how many facial signals of each type occurred together with questions out of the respective signal's total number of occurrences, and we did the same for responses. This included multiple occurrences of facial signals. In the second proportion analysis, we calculated how many out of all questions occurred together with a particular facial signal, and we did the same for responses. In the second proportion analysis, multiple occurrences of a facial signal in a question or response were counted as

in that specific utterance. Thus, while responses may be more likely to have facial signals than questions, when questions do have a signal, they have more of it.

Overall, we found that speakers performed the highest proportion of total frequencies of facial signals in questions and responses for eyebrow frowns, raises, squints, blinks, gaze shifts, and smiles. This is in agreement with previous studies showing links between social actions and different facial signals such as eyebrow movements \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B21-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B22-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B23-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B24-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B25-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B31-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B32-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B33-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B37-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B38-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B42-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B46-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B48-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B49-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B50-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B51-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B52-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B53-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B54-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B55-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B56-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B57-brainsci-11-01017

\], squints \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B37-brainsci-11-01017

\], gaze shifts \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B25-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B39-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B47-brainsci-11-01017

\], and smiles \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B25-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B26-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B27-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B36-brainsci-11-01017

In sum, these findings provide further evidence that these signals may be used to indicate different social actions such as questions and responses; thus, revealing the conversational intention of the speaker. This shows that different facial signals may critically contribute to the communication of social actions in naturalistic conversation, thus forming an integral part of human language.

## Clustering of Facial Signals within Questions and Responses

When analysing clusters, we first observed several co-occurrences between facial signals within questions and responses. Facial signals that frequently co-occurred in both questions and responses were eyebrow frowns and squints, eyebrow raises and eye widenings, and eyebrow raises with smiles. Both blinks and gaze shifts (away from the interlocutor) frequently co-occurred with all other facial signals, but generally co-occurred more with other facial signals in responses. There was a higher number of co-occurrences in questions for eyebrow frowns with blinks, and squints with blinks. However, there was a higher number of co-occurrences in responses for blinks with eyebrow raises, gaze shifts, and smiles. Additionally, there were also more gaze shifts with eyebrow raises and with smiles. In general, the largest number of co-occurrences between facial signals were in responses. Blinks and gaze shifts were the most frequent facial signals overall, so it is not surprising that they co-occurred the most. These results show that there are specific pairwise groupings of co-occurring facial signals that distribute differently in questions versus responses (which may be accompanied by further signals).

To see whether it was possible to distinguish between a question or a response based on facial signals that accompanied them, we used DT models to construct prediction models. Results from the DT models showed that it is possible to statistically predict whether the utterance was a question or a response based on facial signals. Specifically, eyebrow frowns marked questions the most confidently (even if co-occurring with gaze shifts), and gaze shifts with eyebrow raises were predicted to often mark responses. This is in line with studies showing that eyebrow frowns are associated with questions in spoken and signed languages \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B21-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B22-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B23-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B31-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B32-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B33-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B46-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B50-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B51-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B53-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B54-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B55-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B57-brainsci-11-01017

\] and gaze shifts are associated with dispreferred responses \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B47-brainsci-11-01017

\]. It could be that eyebrow frowns often signal social actions that are subclasses of questions, to help potential following speakers to understand the intended message quickly in order to give a timely response \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B9-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B10-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B11-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B12-brainsci-11-01017

\]. The association between gaze shifts and responses may help the speaker indicate that their response to a question will not align with the social action that the form of the question projects \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B47-brainsci-11-01017

\], which may facilitate intention interpretation. Alternatively, it could be that there were more thinking-faces in responses \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B25-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B39-brainsci-11-01017

\], which could be used by the speaker to convey that they want to keep the floor until they have remembered what they were searching for, or may announce that they do not know something. However, it is also possible that at least a proportion of the gaze shifts were used as a turn-taking signal \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B102-brainsci-11-01017

\] or were associated with the cognitive planning of the responses \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B103-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B104-brainsci-11-01017

\]. This analysis demonstrates that a classification of different social actions based on a pool of facial signals as predictors is statistically possible, and that especially eyebrow frowns confidently marked questionhood with and without other facial signals.

After determining that questions and responses could be distinguished based on the co-occurring facial signals, we investigated combinations of facial signals characteristic for questions and responses by using MCA. This analysis indicated that the 12 facial signals could be clustered into eight stable clusters for both questions and responses. Both questions and responses consisted of the following clusters of facial signals: (1) blinks and gaze shifts; (2) eyebrow frowns, squints, and nose wrinkles; (3) eyebrow raises and eye widenings. The remaining facial signals consisted of eyebrow frown raises, eyebrow unilateral raises, pressed lips, mouth corners down, and smiles, each of which did not cluster with any other facial signals. The lack of clustering in the final five signals could be because they frequently associate with multiple facial signals from different clusters and therefore did not reliably fit with any one grouping. The second cluster (i.e., eyebrow frowns, squints, and nose wrinkles) has some resemblance to the facial expression that was previously identified in the literature as the not-face \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B37-brainsci-11-01017

\], which typically consists of eyebrow frowns, compressed chin muscles, and pressed lips, but was also found with squints and nose wrinkles. It could be that the third cluster in questions and responses indicates a 'surprise-face' \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B105-brainsci-11-01017

\], which fits well with our previous observation that this combination of facial signals was more frequent in questions when observing co-occurrences of facial signals (

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#brainsci-11-01017-f004

). Lastly, the first cluster could have originated from the need to close the eyes before moving them to a different position for more stability. These findings show that there are specific constellations of facial signals that occur within both questions and responses, despite questions and responses differing in the frequency with which they were characterised by the occurrence of particular individual signals or signal combinations.

## Timing of Facial Signals within Questions and Responses

Turning now to timing, the majority of facial signals happened early (before or at the very beginning of the verbal utterance). This confirms our hypothesis that facial signals may occur very early in the verbal utterance, or even prior to it, because this is how they may exert the greatest influence on early social action attribution: early visual signals may facilitate quick social action recognition for the following speakers and, thus, quick response planning, which is crucial for tight temporal coordination of conversational turn-taking \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B12-brainsci-11-01017

\]. This especially applies to the highly normed timing when responding to questions, since a longer than average gap may indicate a dispreferred response \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B8-brainsci-11-01017

\]. From a processing perspective, signalling one's intention to ask a question early may therefore be particularly beneficial. Indeed, when looking at the overall distribution of facial signals across social actions, most had an earlier onset in questions compared to responses.

However, some signals occurred relatively late in the utterance too. This was the case for mouth movements, eyebrow frown raises and unilateral brow raises. It may be that the mouth movements were used for sarcastic or ironic intention, such as in studies on spoken and signed languages \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B25-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B26-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B27-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B28-brainsci-11-01017

\]. This intention is typically shown at the end of the utterance for a humoristic effect. The difference in timing of facial signals may also have occurred because of other factors. It could be that some facial signals indicated turn boundaries \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B22-brainsci-11-01017

\] or the begin or end of a topic \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B22-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B24-brainsci-11-01017

\], by either appearing at the start or the end of the speech. Facial signals at the start of the speech could indicate that the speaker intends to take the floor from a previous speaker, or gives the floor to a next speaker if signals are at the end of the utterance. Moreover, facial signals could have occurred at a specific point in the utterance prior to or following cognitive load \[

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B104-brainsci-11-01017

https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8392358/#B106-brainsci-11-01017

\]. However, with the corpus data we have analysed here where many layers of behaviour are inherently intertwined, we cannot tease apart the contributions of these other factors. Future experimental studies are required to tease these possibilities apart. Nevertheless, what we can conclude is that the distribution and timing of facial signal depend highly on the specific social action that is performed, and the patterns the present analysis has revealed are very much in line with a mechanism of early visual signalling which benefits fast social action ascription in conversation.

## Limitations

The current study has some limitations. First, (extreme) laughter heavily affects the visibility of facial signals, since it involves many sub-movements, such as tilting back the head. Therefore, we coded the facial signals from the last evidence or until the first evidence of laughter. Although laughter occurred scarcely, it could be that the artificial cut-offs of facial signals in our data due to the occurrence of laughter still led to small artefacts in our calculation of timing. Second, multiple blinks sometimes occurred with gaps less than or equal to a single video frame (40 ms), which resulted in frames that showed only the white of the eyes, and made it difficult to make accurate annotations about blink onset or offset. Therefore, multiple blinks with gaps less than or equal to one frame were annotated as one blink. This could mean that more blinks were actually performed than we are reporting in this study. However, it could also be that multiple blinks following each other within one frame are perceived as a single communicative unit. Lastly, the present study focused on facial signals during questions and responses in dyadic conversations between acquaintances, but did not look at other categories of conversational social actions, sequential contexts other than question-response sequences, nor different intragroup and intergroup contexts.

## Future Studies

Future studies investigating participants' facial signalling in other dyadic contexts and across different cultures would show whether our findings are representative of questions and response more generally, or if they only apply to dyadic conversations between acquaintances in a particular setting. Additionally, including other social actions or a more fine-grained categorization of the broader social actions of questions and responses investigated here may help to elucidate the extent to which facial signals encode specific social intentions in conversation. It is of course possible—and in fact likely—that the particular patterns identified here are characteristic for certain social actions that questions and responses perform, but not for all of them. Identifying commonalities and differences at these more fine-grained levels of social actions remains an important avenue for future research, as well as trying to disentangle social action from turn-taking signals by looking at different social actions within the same sequential context and vice versa. Moreover, investigating the temporal organization of facial signals within an utterance in relation to one another would help to determine whether there is a fixed order of facial signals that characterises different social actions, including cases where they appear to form social-action-specific clusters of visual signals. In addition, investigating how facial signals temporally synchronise with speech (including prosodic patterns) could show how closely they are aligned during different social actions. Another aspect requiring future research is consideration of the detailed interactional processes that underpin the communication of questions and responses, in particular with respect to the extent to which interlocutors may shape current speakers' facial signalling during questions and responses. Finally, investigating whether the neural signatures differ when social actions are carried by a combination of facial signals and words rather than verbal utterances alone would provide us with important insights into the role of co-speech facial signals during social action recognition.

5. Conclusions

In conclusion, this study showed that questions and responses are characterised by distinct distributions of facial signals, and consist of stable clusters of facial signals. Moreover, most facial signals occurred early in the turn. These findings suggest that specific facial signals, or combinations of facial signals, may be used to indicate different social actions, thus providing visual cues to speakers' social intentions in conversation. The early timing of facial signals could provide a potential facilitative effect of facial signals for social action attribution, which in turn, may help potential following speakers to recognise the speaker's intended message in a timely way during conversation; thus, facilitating fast responding. In sum, the results from this study highlight the potentially important role of the body in the pragmatics of human communication, and provide a foundation for investigating the cognitive and neural basis of social action recognition in face-to-face human communication.

Example themes part 2

Hoeveel privacy heb je nog tegenwoordig?

Tegenwoordig is je telefoon niet meer weg te denken uit het dagelijks leven. Je gebruikt hem onder andere om snel even wat op te zoeken, makkelijk te communiceren met vrienden en als navigatiesysteem. Maar alle apps die je gebruikt verzamelen ook heel veel data over jou. Dit wordt meestal voor positieve doeleinden gebruikt, zoals het opsporen van vermiste mensen of terrorismepreventie. Maar je moet niet vergeten dat systemen de indeling van jouw leven op deze manier heel goed in kaart kunnen brengen. Door locatiegegevens weten ze waar je bent en door zoekgegevens wat je leuk vindt. Verder verdienen bedrijven veel geld door jouw data te verkopen. Hoe sta jij tegenover het verzamelen van persoonlijke informatie? Welke data mag er wel en niet over jou verzameld worden? Hoeveel privacy ben jij bereid om op te offeren ten behoeve van gemak?

Voor- en nadelen van social media

Facebook, Instagram, Twitter en WhatsApp... Social media is overal! Het is handig om snel met je vrienden te kunnen communiceren en om contact te houden met mensen die ver weg wonen en je niet zo vaak meer ziet. Helaas heeft social media ook nadelen, denk bijvoorbeeld aan cyberpesten of het verspreiden van nepnieuws of extremistische ideeën. Verder is er ook een grote kans op social-mediaverslaving. Wat vind jij voor- en nadelen van social media? Overweeg jij weleens je social media accounts te verwijderen? Vind je dat mensen te veel tijd online besteden in plaats van in normale sociale interactie, of maakt dit voor jou niet uit?

Studeren in het Engels of het Nederlands?

Steeds meer universitaire opleidingen worden alleen nog in het Engels aangeboden. Engels is tenslotte de taal van de wetenschap en van het (internationale) bedrijfsleven. Bovendien trekt dit buitenlandse studenten aan, wat zorgt voor internationalisering van de campus. Maar de kwaliteit van het onderwijs gaat er niet per se op vooruit; lang niet alle docenten en studenten kunnen zich net zo goed uitdrukken in het Engels als in het Nederlands. En voor sommige banen is het júist belangrijk dat je je goed kunt uitdrukken in woord en geschrift

in het Nederlands.

Zou jij liever les krijgen in het Engels of in het Nederlands? En ben je bang dat de kwaliteit van het onderwijs hierdoor achteruit gaat? Of denk je dat een Engelstalige opleiding je carrièrekansen juist vergroten?

# From co-speech actions to co-speech gestures? Effects of visibility and information structure on cross-modal synchronization - PUB - Publikationen an der Universität Bielefeld

Making sure you're not a bot!

Making sure you're not a bot!

Difficulty: 5, Speed: 30.639kH/s

Anubis could not load its JavaScript. The server may be overloaded. Please reload the page to try again.

You are seeing this because the administrator of this website has set up Anubis to protect the server against the scourge of AI companies aggressively scraping websites. This can and does cause downtime for the websites, which makes their resources inaccessible for everyone.

Anubis is a compromise. Anubis uses a Proof-of-Work scheme in the vein of Hashcash, a proposed proof-of-work scheme for reducing email spam. The idea is that at individual scales the additional load is ignorable, but at mass scraper levels it adds up and makes scraping much more expensive.

Ultimately, this is a placeholder solution so that more time can be spent on fingerprinting and identifying headless browsers (EG: via how they do font rendering) so that the challenge proof of work page doesn't need to be presented to users that are much more likely to be legitimate.

Please note that Anubis requires the use of modern JavaScript features that plugins like JShelter will disable. Please disable JShelter or other such plugins for this domain.

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION

INTERPRETATIONS OF MESSAGES

The Influence of Various Forms of Equivocation, Face

Concerns, and Sex Differences

RICHARD BELLO

Sam Houston State University

RENEE EDWARDS

Louisiana State University

Equivocation, the use of ambiguity or vagueness, is used to protect face when an interlocu-

tor seeks to avoid a hurtful truth or a deception.This study compared the effects of self and

other-face, sex, and several versions of equivocation on perceptions of messages. Partici-

pants (n = 354) read a scenario about a public-speaking situation in which a classmate

makes a comment about a poor performance. Equivocation, especially that directed at

other-face, is judged more polite than unequivocal criticism. Unequivocal criticism is

judged as more competent and honest than equivocation.A mixed message is regarded the

most positively.

message interpretation; equivocation; face; politeness; communication

The study of indirectness (and, by way of contrast, directness) in

communicative encounters is important and theoretically interesting

for a number of reasons. First, research by discourse analysts (and oth-

ers) that focuses on naturally occurring language in field contexts

strongly suggests that indirectness is a routine and common compo-

nent of everyday exchanges in a variety of social situations (Turner,

Edgley, & Olmstead, 1975). For example, the practice of indirect com-

munication appears to be an issue in contexts as diverse as those that

call for apologies as remediation for norm violations (Blum-Kulka,

House, & Kasper, 1989; Scher & Darley, 1997) and those that feature

the giving of advice in health-care settings (Heritage & Sefi, 1992; Rob-

inson, Shepherd, & Heywood, 1998). The applied linguist Channell

 2005 Sage Publications

(1994) has documented a wide diversity of forms of indirect language

as a part of everyday interaction, and her data suggest as well a variety

of social and psychological reasons for such indirectness (informality

and self-protection, among others).

Second, similar research also suggests the likelihood of more gen-

eral patterns or factors associated with indirectness across a diversity

of contexts. As one example, the level of situational uncertainty

appears possibly to be such a factor. Sharrock and Turner (1978) stud-

ied phone calls to a city police station in the process of examining the

way in which people formulate complaints. These researchers discov-

ered that in situations where the nature of the event being complained

about did not cleanly fall under the heading of police business (and in

that sense, was vague or ambiguous), the complaint calls were likely to

be much less direct and rambling. Sharrock and Turner suggested that

such calls actually fit the nature of the situation better and helped to

legitimize the complaint as one that might be handled in some manner

by the police.Similarly,Rummelhart (1983), in examining communica-

tion during interviews of individuals living within an institutional set-

ting, found that these individuals routinely varied the level of clarity of

their responses depending upon the nature of the setting, especially

how well they understood the context of the interaction. The more

uncertain they were about that context, the more indirect were their

responses. Another example of a general factor associated with indi-

rectness is that of face. Goffman (1967), in his ethnographic studies of

human interaction across a variety of settings, was one of the first

scholars to routinely make use of the concept of face, the part of self we

present to others for social approval, to explain that interaction. He

also argued (1967) that we attempt to manage the face that we present

to others by taking certain lines, that is, making use of an appropriate

set of verbal and nonverbal behaviors.One key implication of this work

is that one of the ways we manage face is by varying the degree of

directness of our messages, for example, glossing over, redefining, or

playing down behaviors (of self and other) that threaten face. Such

intentional use of indirect verbal behavior is often referred to as

equivocation.

EQUIVOCATION AND FACE CONCERNS

A parent asks a son or daughter (who is a college student) about the

quality of a recent classroom performance, and the student replies, “It

was an interesting experience.” When the parent asks about the per-

formance of one of the student’s friends,a fellow classmate, the student

says, “It was fun to watch.” The student has equivocated in both situa-

tions but has done so in very different ways and perhaps for different

reasons. Recent research (Edwards & Bello, 2001) has examined such

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 161

equivocation along with the issue of whose face is being protected by

the equivocation, that of the self (in this example, the student himself

or herself) or the other (the student’s classmate). The present study

proposes to revisit these face concerns in the use of equivocal (versus

clear) communication, as well as to extend this focus by looking at the

impact of various versions of equivocation.

Equivocation is the intended use of unclear messages, that is, vague,

ambiguous, or nonstraightforward communication (Bavelas, Black,

Chovil, & Mullett, 1990a; Turner et al., 1975), often occurring in

response to difficult or awkward situations. One significant line of

research treats equivocation as a response to avoidance-avoidance

conflict (AAC), in which a communicator is faced with two apparently

direct but unattractive communicative choices (e.g., having to choose

between outright deceit and a hurtful or embarrassing truth) (Bavelas,

1983, 1985, 1998; Bavelas, Black, Bryson, & Mullett, 1988; Bavelas

et al., 1990a, 1990b; Bavelas & Chovil, 1986; Bavelas & Smith, 1982).

In these situations, the choice is almost always to equivocate rather

than to lie or tell the truth. Bavelas and her colleagues have persua-

sively argued that equivocation can be viewed as avoiding or

deemphasizing at least one of the basic elements that make up any

communicative attempt: sender, receiver, content, and context (see

especially Bavelas et al., 1990a; Bavelas & Smith, 1996).

Equivocation has been linked with the protection of face, especially

when it is motivated by avoidance-avoidance conflict. Brown and

Levinson (1978) suggested that, in order to save face, individuals often

adopt a politeness strategy that may involve sending less direct, more

equivocal hedges. Others have catalogued the ways in which people

use tactful messages by sidestepping explicit disagreement to save

face (Bull, 1998; Bull, Elliott, Palmer, & Walker, 1996; Cappella &

Palmer, 1992). Some researchers have reconstituted AAC in different

terms, such as tact situations (Motley, 1992), or used the more funda-

mental concept of face as a way of explaining why AAC so routinely

leads to equivocation (Bull et al., 1996).

Although research on equivocation has been linked to face concerns,

it has generally not explicated the relationship between equivocation

and various forms of face. Brown and Levinson (1978) discussed four

types of face (positive and negative face of self and other) and the face-

threatening acts that address them. Of special interest to recent stud-

ies (Edwards & Bello, 2001) and to the present study is criticism,which

Brown and Levinson (1978) argue is a threat to the positive face of the

person being criticized.Lim and Bowers (1991) proposed that individu-

als have three types of face wants: fellowship face, autonomy face, and

competence face. The first refers to “the want to be included” whereas

the second is “the want not to be imposed on.” The third, competence

face, refers to “the want that their abilities be respected” (p. 420) and is

threatened by criticism. It is addressed by displays of “approbation,”

162 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

attempts to emphasize the strengths and abilities of the hearer and to

minimize the hearer’s shortcomings.

Methodologically, equivocation research has often relied on scenar-

ios in which a participant is asked to offer some sort of criticism to

another individual—for example, concerning a poor performance on a

class presentation, unattractive appearance, or a poorly chosen gift

(Bavelas et al., 1990a). In these cases, the equivocal messages used by

the participants can be thought of as polite forms or as approbation

designed to protect the competence face of the other individual. Some

other equivocation research has used scenarios in which an individual

is asked to describe his or her own weak performance—for example,

concerning a poor GPA or being fired at work (Bello, 1999). In these

cases, the equivocal messages are attempts by individuals to protect

their own competence face and may be forms of self-approbation.

Whereas equivocal messages that concern another’s poor perfor-

mance are instances of politeness, what of equivocal messages that

concern poor performance by the self? Edwards and Bello (2001)

argued that they are also instances of politeness. Politeness consists of

attempts to protect the face of another. If one individual tells another

individual of his or her problems, weaknesses, or shortcomings (using

clear rather than equivocal language), the other individual is conse-

quently constrained to respond, perhaps to dispute the assessment,

offer comfort, or provide support, but certainly not to ignore the prob-

lem. In using clear language, then, the individual constrains the auton-

omy face of the other and is less polite than when she or he uses equivo-

cal language. Thus, equivocal language that protects self-face may

simultaneously protect the face of the other and constitute an expres-

sion of politeness, a notion that is at odds with the thinking of Brown

and Levinson (1978) and others (e.g., Rogan & Hammer, 1994). How-

ever, the notion that utterances address only one type of face at a time

has been disputed by a number of scholars (Lim & Bowers, 1991; Wil-

son, Aleman, & Leatham, 1998). Ting-Toomey and Cocroft (1994) pro-

posed that a category of “ ‘mutual face’ should be added to the self-face

and other-face dimension” (p. 323).

RESPONSES TO MESSAGES

One way that researchers have examined the issues of face and

equivocation is to consider how communicators interpret equivocal

messages of others and how they perceive the individuals who use

them. Interpretation of messages refers to the connotative and denota-

tive meanings attributed to a message, including how the target views

the intentions of the source (Edwards, 1998). Recent research

(Edwards & Bello,2001) has investigated four dimensions of responses

to equivocal messages. At issue in the present study is whether these

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 163

dimensions vary as a function of different versions of equivocation, self

versus other-face concerns, or a combination of them.

The first dimension is the denotative level of meaning. When an

individual equivocates, he or she does so (obviously) to avoid a clear

response.When a friend describes a gift you have given him or her with

an equivocation, for example, he or she is doing so in order to avoid

answering the question, “How do you like it?”But what assumptions do

people make when they hear the equivocal response? Do they assume

that the gift was a good selection or unappreciated? Similarly, other

equivocal messages allow listeners to make assumptions about mean-

ing at the content level. When an individual is asked to evaluate a per-

formance but gives an equivocal response, the listeners may make

assumptions about the quality of the performance. In their investiga-

tion of this issue, Edwards and Bello (2001) did not find any difference

in denotative meaning between one equivocal message (“It was inter-

esting”) and an unequivocal message (“I/You messed up”).

A second response to equivocation concerns perceptions of honesty.

Honesty, one of the instrumental values identified by Rokeach (1973),

refers to being sincere and truthful. Language users value honesty and

truthfulness, as Grice (1975, 1981) recognized with the conversational

maxim of quality (be truthful). The Bavelas group has proposed that

individuals in avoidance-avoidance conflict use equivocal messages to

avoid clear, hurtful truths but also to avoid being deceptive (Bavelas

et al., 1990a, 1990b). Thus, equivocation is theoretically conceptual-

ized by the Bavelas group as truthful but unclear communication

designed largely to lessen the negative impact (upon self or other or

both) of difficult or awkward messages. Edwards and Bello (2001)

found, however, that communicators regard equivocation as less

honest than unequivocal language.

The perception of politeness, another of Rokeach’s (1973) instru-

mental values, represents the third dimension used in responding to

equivocation. Previous research has shown that messages are per-

ceived to vary in politeness, which is the degree to which they protect

the face of the other (Baxter, 1984; Trees & Manusov, 1998; Wilson

et al., 1998). Equivocal messages that are designed to protect the face

of the other are recognizably instances of politeness, as are equivocal

messages that primarily protect the face of the self. Previous research

(Edwards & Bello, 2001) found that equivocal communication is

regarded as more polite than unequivocal communication, with equiv-

ocal messages that protect other-face judged to be the most polite, and

those that unequivocally criticize other-face as the least polite. In addi-

tion, that research found that women judge equivocation as more

polite than do men, supporting other analyses of sex differences and

politeness (Crosby & Nyquist, 1977; Lakoff, 1973; McMillan, Clifton,

McGrath, & Gale, 1977).

164 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

The fourth dimension is communicator competence. Previous

research has shown that message targets make assessments of the

communication competence of message sources. Communication com-

petence is conceptualized as the degree to which individuals are able to

accomplish interpersonal goals while maintaining the face of fellow

communicators (Wiemann,1977). Because both notions include a focus

on protecting the face of a communication partner, communication

competence is related to politeness. However, communication compe-

tence is a broader construct in that it encompasses the notion of goal

attainment. Bavelas and her colleagues argue that equivocation is a

competent response to an avoidance-avoidance situation. Consistent

with this,Edwards and Bello (2001) originally predicted that equivoca-

tion is perceived as more competent than unequivocal criticism. Inter-

estingly, they failed to support that prediction in Study 1, with the

results suggesting that the opposite was true. A second data collection,

reported as Study 2, confirmed the first: Equivocation is judged as less

competent than unequivocal communication.

LIMITATIONS OF EXISTING RESEARCH

One of the key shortcomings of previous research on interpretations

of equivocal communication is that it compared only one version of

equivocation (“It was interesting”) with unequivocal criticism

(Edwards & Bello, 2001). A question remains whether the previous

findings can be substantiated across a variety of equivocal message

versions, enhancing their generalizability and external validity. This

is a special concern for the finding that equivocation is generally

competent than unequivocal criticism, considering that

this finding runs counter to the claim of the Bavelas group that equivo-

cation is essentially competent communication (see especially,Bavelas

et al., 1990a). This claim of the Bavelas group was never an empirical

finding, as it was not tested in their research.

There are at least two specific competing rationales that might

explain the finding that unequivocal criticism is judged to be more

competent than equivocation. First, as we suspect is true, Grice’s

(1975, 1981) conversational maxim of manner (clarity) could explain

the finding in that it proposes that communicators generally strive to

be understood, in some cases even at the risk of damaging self or other-

face. In other words, perhaps concerns about clarity often trump con-

cerns about saving face, at least regarding

perceptions

of the compe-

tence of communicators who wrestle with those concerns.Thus,assum-

ing that the results found by Edwards and Bello (2001) for competence

were due primarily to differences in equivocation per se, then those

results should stay basically the same whatever version of

equivocation is used.

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 165

On the other hand, a second rationale explaining the finding is that

perceptions of competence might have been due to a lack of subtlety (or

some other message characteristic) of the “It was interesting” equivo-

cal response. If this or a similar rationale obtains, then different equiv-

ocal responses should produce different (perhaps stronger) percep-

tions of competence. For example, perhaps “It was okay, but there were

things that could be improved” (a message scored as equivocal by the

Bavelas group’s judges—see Bavelas et al., 1990a) would be perceived

as more competent than “It was interesting.” In either case, employing

a variety of equivocal responses in the present study should help to

determine which of these rationales best applies, as well as provide

insight into the external validity of all previous findings.

Thus, the present investigation examines the influence of multiple

versions of equivocation along with face on perceptions of denotative

meaning, honesty, politeness, and communication competence. Previ-

ous research (Edwards & Bello, 2001) found no difference in the deno-

tative meanings of the single equivocation (“It was interesting”) and

unequivocal criticism. We address this issue with the following

research question:

Research Question (RQ) 1:Do several versions of equivocal and unequivocal messages have different denotative meanings?

Past research (Edwards & Bello, 2001) found that equivocation is

perceived to be less honest, more polite, and less competent than un-

equivocal criticism. This research also determined that equivocation

interacts with face concerns, such that messages that protect other-

face are judged as most polite, those that attack other-face are judged

least polite, and those that protect or attack self-face are perceived as

intermediate in politeness. We propose the following hypotheses test-

ing across various forms of equivocation:

Hypothesis 1 (H1): Equivocal communication, across several versions, is perceived to be less honest than unequivocal criticism.

H2: Equivocal communication, across several versions, is perceived to be more polite than unequivocal criticism.

H3: Equivocal communication, across several versions, is perceived to be less competent than unequivocal criticism.

H4: Equivocation and face concerns interact so that several versions of equivocal messages that protect the other are perceived as most polite, whereas messages that criticize the other are perceived as least polite, and messages that protect or criticize self-face are intermediate in politeness.

Finally, the previous study examined sex differences in perceptions

of equivocation and found that women perceive it to be more polite

than do men. We test for this effect across the versions of equivocation

with the following prediction:

166 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

H5: Women perceive equivocal communication, across several versions, as more polite than do men.

The preceding hypotheses and research question compare unequiv-

ocal criticism and equivocation (across several versions). Thus, the

first stage of this research compares the two types of messages by com-

bining the versions of equivocation into one category and comparing it

to unequivocal criticism.However,additional insight into equivocation

may be provided by testing the equivocal messages individually. This

will reveal whether interlocutors respond to all equivocal messages in

essentially the same way or whether variations in wording influence

perceptions of denotative meaning, honesty, politeness, or communica-

tion competence. The second stage of this research compares the ver-

sions of equivocation based on the following research question:

RQ2: What is the effect of the version of equivocation (and its possible interaction with face and sex) on perceptions of denotative meaning, honesty, politeness, and communication competence?

PARTICIPANTS

The participants

= 354) were students enrolled in public speak-

ing classes at a large southern university. The sample included 161

males (46%) and 191 females (54%), and consisted of 10% freshmen,

54% sophomores, 27% juniors, and 18% seniors. A total of 46% of the

sample were age 18 to 19,25% were 20,13% were 21,13% were 22 to 25,

and 3% were 26 and over. For ethnic/racial background, 62% were

European American, 17% African American, 7% Hispanic, 3% Asian,

and 12% other.Almost all (97%) of the participants were U.S.citizens.

GENERAL PROCEDURE

Class instructors naïve to the purpose of the study administered a

questionnaire to students during class time. A total of 24 versions of

the questionnaire were randomly intermixed and distributed to partic-

ipants who used computer scan forms to record their responses. Partic-

ipation was voluntary and anonymous.

The questionnaire presented a scenario adapted from the works of

Bavelas et al. (1990a), Bello (1999), and Edwards and Bello (2001). It

described a situation in which one student in a public-speaking class

asks another student about the quality of a speech that was delivered.

The second student responds with either an equivocation or unequivo-

cal criticism. Participants, who were instructed to imagine themselves

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 167

as involved in the scenario, then responded to Likert-scaled items that

assessed the dependent variables: perception of the speech and percep-

tions of the honesty, politeness, and communication competence of the

speaker of the equivocal message or unequivocal criticism. In addition,

participants rated the clarity of the message.

PREDICTOR VARIABLES

The predictor variables were (a) the level of equivocation in the mes-

sage, (b) face concerns, and (c) sex of the participant. The sex of the

source of the message was included as a control variable. Level of

equivocation was manipulated by presenting two versions of the mes-

sage in the scenario. In the “Unequivocal Criticism” condition, the

classmate said, “I/You messed up.” Although many variations of

unequivocal criticism are possible, Edwards and Bello (2001) used this

particular message, providing a basis for comparing the results of the

present study to the previous investigation.

In the “Equivocation” condition, the classmate used one of the fol-

lowing messages to describe the speech: (a) “It was interesting,” (b)

“Not bad,” (c) “I thought it sounded alright,” (d) “It was okay,” or (e) “It

was okay, but there were things that could be improved.” Edwards and

Bello (2001) used the first message, “it was interesting,” whereas the

other messages were taken directly from the work of Bavelas and her

colleagues. Bavelas (1983) used “Not bad” as a forced-choice option for

equivocation in her early research on AAC. We judged it to be a response

that our participants would find appropriate. For the remaining three

responses, we selected actual responses written by individuals pre-

sented with a similar scenario in research by Bavelas and Chovil

(1986). Of the seven messages scored as equivocal, we chose three of

the four with the highest scores in equivocation.1 Bavelas and Chovil

(1986) scored the messages using a system in which positive scores

represent equivocation and negative scores are unequivocal. Of the

messages from Bavelas included in this study, “Not bad” scored 4.36, “I

thought it sounded alright” scored .82, “It was okay” scored 3.71, and

“It was okay,but there were things that could be improved”scored 2.38.

These messages satisfy the three criteria proposed by Jackson and

Jacobs (1983) for research on messages and language. First, the mes-

sages include prototypical examples of equivocation (“Not bad” and “It

was interesting”). Second, the messages represent a variety of forms of

equivocation, and third, naturalness was maximized by taking three

messages directly from statements written by participants in previous

The second predictor variable, face concerns, refers to whose face is

most threatened or addressed by the message. This variable was

manipulated by presenting two versions of the scenario. In the “Self-

Face” version, the speaker is describing his or her own speech when he

168 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

or she equivocates or says “I messed up.” In the “Other-Face” condition,

the speaker is describing the imagined speech of the participant. For

the third predictor variable, sex,participants identified their sex in the

demographic section of the questionnaire. Finally, sex of source, the

control variable, referred to the source of the equivocal or unequivocal

message. The scenarios were worded so that the classmate was either

male or female.

Each scenario described the student as performing poorly on the

speech. In the previous study, Edwards and Bello (2001) compared sce-

narios in which the speech was described as being poorly done to sce-

narios that included no description of the speech. Respondents rated

the speech more negatively in the “poor speech” condition but this

effect did not interact with any other relationships between the vari-

ables. Table 1 presents the scenarios used in the study.

DEPENDENT VARIABLES

The four dependent variables were perceptions of (a) the speech

(denotative meaning), (b) honesty, (c) politeness, and (d) communica-

tion competence. All variables were measured with Likert-type items

using a 5-point scale ranging from

strongly agree

strongly disagree.

Each scale had one or more reverse-coded items in order to avoid

response bias. Perception of the speech (denotative meaning) was mea-

sured with six items: the speech was great, the speech was poorly done,

the speech should earn a good grade, the speech sets a high standard

for other students, it was a bad speech, and the speech was very suc-

cessful (Cronbach’s alpha = .80 for this study).

Honesty, politeness, and communication competence were mea-

sured using a total of 25 items patterned after the measurement of

relational communication (Burgoon & Hale, 1987). Honesty was mea-

sured with eight items: this person (the classmate) is honest, I trust

this person, this person is deceitful, this person is misleading, the per-

son tells the truth, this person is willing to speak his/her mind, this

person is deceptive, this person says what he/she thinks (Cronbach’s

alpha = .83).Politeness was measured with eight items measuring gen-

eral politeness (see Trees & Manusov, 1998): this person is very polite,

this person is rude, this person was trying to say the right thing, this

person was trying to protect everyone’s feelings, this person is negative

toward me, this person says the right thing at the right time, this per-

concerned about my feelings, this person is appropriate in

his/her comments (Cronbach’s alpha = .72). Finally, communication

competence was measured with nine items: this person is a good com-

municator, this person is

good at giving feedback, this person is a

poor communicator, this person is good at expressing thoughts, this

person is a skillful communicator, this person is an ineffective commu-

nicator, this person manages communication well, this person is an

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 169

inept communicator, this person is a competent communicator

(Cronbach’s alpha = .86).

DATA ANALYSIS

The data were analyzed in two stages using the multivariate proce-

dure of the general linear model with four dependent variables. In the

first stage, the predictor variables were equivocation (2 levels), face

concerns (2 levels), sex of the participant (2 levels), and sex of the

source (2 levels). In the second stage, equivocation was tested with six

levels and the other predictors remained the same. All of the predictor

variables were between-groups variables. Because the equivocal mes-

sages were not randomly drawn from a population of equivocations,

they were treated as fixed effects rather than as random effects

(Jackson & Jacobs, 1983).

The first stage of the analysis combined the five variations of equiv-

ocation into one category and compared it to unequivocal criticism.

Relevant to the hypotheses, the multivariate analysis revealed a sig-

nificant main effect for equivocation along with a significant interac-

tion effect for equivocation by face.2 The following section describes the

significant multivariate effect and the significant univariate tests

associated with it. The multivariate test for equivocation was signifi-

cant, Wilks’s Λ = .809,

The second research question addressed the effect of the version of

equivocation (and its possible interaction with face and sex) on percep-

competence. This second stage of analysis included six levels of equivo-

cation along with the other predictor variables.

Relevant to the hypothesized relationships, the multivariate analy-

sis revealed a significant main effect for equivocation and a significant

interaction effect for equivocation by face.4 The following section

describes each significant multivariate effect and the significant

univariate tests associated with it. Table 2 presents the means, stan-

This study examined the effects of several versions of equivocation

and unequivocal criticism directed at self and other-face on percep-

tions of denotative meaning, honesty, politeness, and communicator

competence. Consistent with our hypotheses, we found that unequivo-

cal criticism is perceived to be honest (although similar in honesty to

one equivocal message), impolite, and competent (although less so

than one equivocal message). Unequivocal criticism directed at other-

face is the least polite message, and equivocation directed at other-face

is perceived as the most polite. Contrary to our prediction, we did not

find that females judge equivocation as more polite than do males.

In regard to our first research question, we found an effect for the

topic of unequivocal criticism to be judged more negatively than the

topic of equivocation. Regarding the second research question, we

found that one equivocation in particular, “It was okay, but there were

than the other equivocations. These findings extend previous work on

perceptions of equivocation in interesting ways.

Unequivocal criticism is generally perceived as honest and compe-

tent communication, albeit impolite. This research supports the work

of Edwards and Bello (2001) by extending the generalizability of the

previous investigations: The findings are not an artifact of the contex-

tually loaded phrase, “It was interesting,” but rather extend across a

variety of equivocations. The finding that unequivocal criticism is

judged as honest makes intuitive sense—a dishonest criticism is prob-

ably a rare occurrence except in a joking or teasing situation. Similarly,

Edwards and Bello (2001) and others (Bull, 1998; Cappella & Palmer,

1992) have noted that equivocation is a form of polite communication

because it protects face. Thus, equivocation protecting other-face is

judged the most polite and unequivocal criticism that attacks other-

face is the least polite, with self-face-directed comments showing mod-

erate levels of politeness. The rationale laid out earlier would also sug-

gest that equivocation protecting self-face should be perceived as more

polite than unequivocal criticism of self (in the sense that such criti-

cism would likely constrain autonomy face of the other), and a careful

inspection of the means in Table 3 supports precisely this notion.

In contrast, the finding that unequivocal criticism is judged as more

competent than equivocation is more intuitively surprising. Bavelas

et al. (1990a) argued that equivocation is a

response to a dif-

ficult situation, in part because it is the preferred response in situa-

tions of avoidance-avoidance conflict. The notion that equivocation is

competent was not an empirically verified finding of Bavelas’s research,

but rather an explanatory inference helpful in discussing why equivo-

cation should be so prevalent and varied. Even so, it might be expected

that unequivocal criticism would be judged empirically as less compe-

tent than equivocation, given Bavelas’s explanation. However, the cur-

rent study confirms the work of Edwards and Bello (2001) by finding

that unequivocal criticism is judged as more competent than equivoca-

tion. As such, it lends more support to the first of the two competing

rationales explaining the previous findings on perceptions of compe-

tence, that the findings are due primarily to differing perceptions of

equivocation versus unequivocal criticism per se and, therefore, hold

across a variety of versions of equivocation. We suspect that three pro-

cesses are in operation. The first is that the conversational principle of

clarity is a more important concern for communicators than Bavelas

recognized. Second, interactants may value honesty more than polite-

ness. Finally, we suspect that individuals sometimes reward others for

expressing especially difficult messages—for example, telling some-

one that he or she performed poorly.

This study did not find that females judge equivocation as more

polite than do males, in contrast to earlier investigations (Edwards &

176 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

Bello, 2001). The present study used several versions of equivocation

rather than just “it was interesting.” One explanation for the different

finding is that women may be more sensitive to the interpretation of

that particular message than men are, but that the difference does not

hold across a variety of equivocations. Another difference is that the

previous study also examined the role of ego-involvement and half of

the scenarios involved conversations that were overheard. Women

may have empathized more than men in the “overheard” scenarios,

leading to sex differences in judgments. Perhaps men and women dif-

fer in their judgments when a message is directed at someone else, but

do not differ when the messages are directed at themselves (as in the

present study).

This study suggests that denotative meaning varies as a function of

equivocation. When a classmate states, “I/You messed up,” the recipi-

ent assumes that the speech was poorly done.Equivocal responses lead

to more positive evaluations of the speech, with “Not bad” receiving the

most positive response. However, the highest mean is only 2.71 on a 5-

point scale, revealing that even equivocations produce fairly negative

judgments of the topic. Interactants are well aware that equivocation

is a response to an avoidance-avoidance conflict and that the source of

an equivocation is trying to avoid a hurtful “truth.” The finding that

denotative meaning changes as a function of equivocation varies from

previous research (Edwards & Bello, 2001), which found no differences

across two data collections with large samples in each group.We specu-

late, therefore, that the different finding might simply be anomalous,

might be due to methodological differences discussed above, or might

be due to changes in social and political climate.Edwards and Bello col-

lected data when President Clinton was going through impeachment

hearings. At that time, the media and various humorists were focusing

on the president’s use of equivocation in his responses to the charges,

which may have sensitized interactants, at least temporarily, to the

meanings that can be expressed (or concealed) with equivocation.

One of the equivocal messages in this study produced results at

variance with the other equivocations. Version five, “It was okay, but

there were things that could be improved,” was judged to be very polite,

very honest, and more competent than any other message. Although

Bavelas rated this message as equivocal, her group’s research sug-

gested that it was less equivocal than two of the messages we have

included here, “It was okay” and “Not bad” (Bavelas et al., 1990a). A

closer examination of this message reveals that it contains two

thought units connected by “but.” The first unit (“It was okay”) is

clearly an equivocation whereas the second unit (“There were things

that could be improved”) appears substantially less equivocal. Bavelas

and colleagues referred to this and similar messages as “internally

contradictory . . . messages \[that\] often begin with a positive phrase,

Bello, Edwards / EQUIVOCATION AND MESSAGE INTERPRETATION 177

followed by a criticism” (p. 101). Furthermore, connecting the two units

with “but” seems to place the emphasis on the second unit. Recipients

of this message, however, appear to respond to both units—recognizing

the politeness of the first, the honesty of the second, and perhaps the

competence inherent in the combination. Interestingly, Bavelas’s com-

ment that these kinds of messages “seem to reflect directly the conflict

in the situation faced by the respondents” (p. 104) might just be one

important reason why the example in our study was perceived so posi-

tively. An extension of this finding would be to examine a reversal of

the message: “There were things that could be improved, but it was

okay.”Would placing the more equivocal (and less critical) thought unit

second serve to change how the message is perceived, perhaps

lowering perceptions of competence and politeness, but increasing

The purpose of this investigation was to examine the effects of self

and other-face perceptions of equivocation, across several versions of

equivocation. Given the connection between deception and equivoca-

tion, it would also be worthwhile in future research to construct a sce-

nario version that includes an outright lie (in addition to unequivocal

criticism and equivocation). It might also prove fruitful to examine the

relationship of equivocation to factors such as relational satisfaction

and relational maintenance (rather than only face and face-saving,

which are more focused on individual concerns).

Our original intent was not to parse messages or identify particular

wording for criticism to be judged more effective. However, our results

offer some interesting conclusions in that regard by suggesting that

negative, evaluative messages may be categorized broadly as unequiv-

ocal criticism, equivocation, and mixed. Unequivocal criticism leads to

the most negative interpretation of that which is being evaluated and

perhaps should be used when critics truly want recipients to under-

stand the weakness of their performance. It is judged to be very honest

and competent, but low in politeness, especially when directed at

another individual. Equivocation is judged to be the most polite mes-

sage, especially when directed at other-face, and perhaps should be

used by critics when protecting face is the highest goal. Finally, mixed

messages, those that contain elements of equivocation and unequivo-

cal criticism, may represent the “ideal” criticism. Although they do not

communicate the negative meaning as clearly as unequivocal criti-

cism, they appear to be perceived as very honest, very polite, and the

most competent. Of course, we offer these conclusions as directions for

future research rather than as firm conclusions from the current inves-

tigation.We did not construct version five of the equivocations to repre-

sent a “mixed” message per se but suggest that future research pursue

this line of questioning. Just as in the present study we attempted to

solidify and better generalize an earlier finding by using several

178 JOURNAL OF LANGUAGE AND SOCIAL PSYCHOLOGY / June 2005

different instantiations of equivocation, it would make sense in future

studies to accomplish a similar goal by incorporating several versions

of mixed messages, some or all of which could be drawn from a set of

naturally occurring language data.

Indirectness in communication continues to be a meaningful and

fruitful area of research that would appear to benefit from the use of

varied perspectives and methodologies.The line of research pursued in

the present experimental study has certainly been informed, both con-

ceptually and empirically, by the work of those who examine language

and communication as they occur in more natural situations (see

above). It is our hope that any additional insight gained from this study

into the general tendencies of interpretation of equivocal messages can

inform those interested in naturally occurring language of, for exam-

ple, what to look for in examining the specifics of how interactants pro-

duce and (especially) respond to indirect messages. In addition, dis-

course analysts, in examining a corpus of temporally distributed

language data, appear to be in a good position to extend this research

by noticing structural patterns of equivocation within ongoing conver-

sations. For example, do more equivocal criticisms and evaluations

tend to precede less equivocal ones and, if so, is such a pattern modified

or reversed by how these evaluations are interpreted or by the specifics

of the social situation? Such a possibility is at least suggested by one

finding of an earlier experimental study (Bello, 2000), which showed

that some individuals (specifically, those with low ambiguity

tolerance) chose to use more equivocal messages in the opening phase

of a conversation than in the middle phase.

1. The other message scoring high on equivocation by Bavelas and Chovil (1986) was

“not so hot.” Because this message offers a negative evaluation of the speech, we deemed

it sufficiently different from the other messages and therefore did not include it in our

study. Future research might investigate equivocal messages that are more positive or

negative in their orientation.

2. Unrelated to the hypotheses, we found a significant main effect for face and signifi-

cant interaction effects for sex by source and for equivocation by sex by source. These did

not influence the interpretations of the hypothesized relationships. Details are available

from the authors.

3. Unrelated to the hypotheses, we also found a significant effect for the face by equiv-

ocation interaction on honesty. Details are available from the authors.

4. Unrelated to the hypotheses, we also found significant main effects for face and sex

as well as a significant interaction effect on honesty for equivocation by face. Details are

available from the authors.

5. We report this “marginally significant” effect because the η 2

is greater than .03.

Richard Bello is an associate professor in the Department of Speech Communica-

tion at Sam Houston State University. His research interests are currently focused

on both the causes of interpersonal equivocation and how equivocation plays a role

in the perception and evaluation of messages and message sources.

Renee Edwards is professor and chair in the Department of Communication Stud-

ies at Louisiana State University. Her research interests include factors (such as

personality) that influence how messages are interpreted in interpersonal commu-

nication, as well as the role of gender in interaction and self-presentation.
